# Transcription of 2505.07923v4.pdf

Transcription of `2505.07923v4.pdf` in this directory, 20 pages; each `## PDF page` heading marks a page of the PDF.

Method: no page was sent to OCR; pages 1-20 from the PDF's own text layer (pdftotext version 26.01.0 with the TeX glyph-name table sha256 964f0780f6b9, one page at a time). No page was compared against its rendered image; OCR errors remain where a scan is unclear, so the PDF is the authority for every quotation.

## PDF page 1

A Superalgebra Within:
representations of
lightest standard model particles
form a Z52 -graded algebra

arXiv:2505.07923v4 [hep-ph] 9 Jun 2026

N. Furey
Iris Adlershof, Humboldt-Universität zu Berlin,
Zum Grossen Windkanal 2, Berlin, Germany
and
AIMS South Africa, 6 Melrose Road,
Muizenberg, Cape Town, South Africa
and
Nicolaus Copernicus University,
Grudziadzka 5, Toruń, Poland
furey@physik.hu-berlin.de
HU-EP-25/16
It is demonstrated how a set of particle representations, familiar from the Standard Model, collectively form a superalgebra. Those representations mirroring the internal behaviour of the Standard
Model’s gauge bosons, and three generations of fermions, are each included in this algebra, with
exception only to those irreps involving the top quark. This superalgebra is isomorphic to the Euclidean Jordan algebra of 16 × 16 hermitian matrices, H16 (C), and is generated by division algebras.
The division algebraic substructure enables a natural factorization between internal and spacetime
symmetries. It also allows for the definition of a Z52 grading on the algebra. Those internal symmetries respecting this substructure are found to be su(3)C ⊕ su(2)L ⊕ u(1)Y , in addition to four
iterations of u(1). For spatial symmetries, one finds multiple copies of so(3). Given its Jordan
algebraic foundation, and its apparent non-relativistic character, the model may supply a bridge
between particle physics and quantum computing.
We close by describing current research directions. These include (1) detailing how this construction fits into the larger picture of Bott Periodic Particle Physics, first introduced in [1], [2], [3], (2)
investigating how the origin of this Peirce decomposition might be grounded in the unsung algebra
O ⊕ H ⊕ C ⊕ R, and (3) proposing how these two directions may merge by reframing the model in
terms of the 16 R dimensional sedenion algebra, S.

This paper is written with the intention of being accessible to a broad range of physicists and mathematicians.
Its central mathematical structure is that of hermitian
matrices.
I.

A Z2 GRADING WITHIN

High-energy physicists have long hoped that particle
detectors would expose a certain 1-to-1 correspondence
between the bosons and fermions of elementary particle
physics, [4]. However, as of the time of this writing, the
LHC has failed to confirm the prediction in its usual incarnation. In this article, we propose that it may not
be necessary to forfeit the idea in its entirety. That is,
instead of searching outside the Standard Model for the
desired Z2 grading, it may be possible to search within.
As with SUSY models, the model proposed in this article is based on the structure of a superalgebra. However,
we use this superalgebra in a different way, and as a result, find a description of elementary particles that is not
supersymmetric. Notably, this description does not entail a doubling of the Standard Model’s particles.
It is rarely emphasized in the literature, if at all, that

the ratio of the number of fermionic degrees of freedom
to bosonic degrees of freedom in the Standard Model is
approximately 3:1. We observe that if one were to recast the third generation of fermions as a naı̈vely bosonic
product ∼ ΨΨ† , then this ratio, incidentally, becomes
1:1.
In this article, we do indeed identify a Z2 grading
within the Standard Model’s existing irreps, not only
once, however, but several times over. This Z52 grading makes obvious a (64 + 3 · 64) R partitioning of the
256 R dimensional H16 (C). Each of the Standard Model’s
known irreps are included in this minimalistic superalgebra, with exception only to those representations involving the top quark. As a result, one might speculate
that the top quark is fully composite in nature, while the
bottom quark is partially composite, [5], [6], [7]. For a
preview see Figure 5.
As an alternative perspective, we argue that there may
be a way to introduce all third generation states via
a product, reminiscent of the spinor-helicity formalism.
This H16 (C) superalgebra, after all, may be viewed as a
direct extension of H2 (C), wherein familiar Weyl operators, pµ σ µ , reside.
As will be seen towards the end of this article, the

## PDF page 2

2
spatial symmetries respecting the division algebraic Z52
grading lend themselves to an interpretation that matter particles are extended objects. Intuitively, they are
the “square root” of pµ σ µ , or rather, the full momentumspace covariant derivative. The model does not appear
to require that these objects be embedded into a background spacetime.
II.

IN CONTEXT

Upon a first glance at the Standard Model’s list of
fundamental particles, it might be difficult to see much
more than randomness. Why such a long, and erratic
sequence of irreducible representations?
However, patterns do unquestionably exist. To be explicit, the Standard Model’s fermions are known to come
in three copies, or generations. As mentioned above, its
fermion-to-boson ratio is roughly 3:1. Furthermore, we
know that SU(2)L violates parity maximally. Our failure
to understand the bigger picture is not a proof that no
such bigger picture exists.
Upon the inclusion of three generations of right-handed
neutrinos, the total number of local off-shell (unconstrained) degrees of freedom in the Standard Model
comes to approximately 250 R, that is, depending on how
the Higgs is included, and on how Weyl operators are
handled. Given a dearth of new particle discoveries at
the LHC, it may be advisable to search for mathematical
structures of this same size. Intriguingly, a few candidates do readily emerge. For example, the exceptional
Lie algebra e8 occupies 248 R, while the Clifford algebra Cl(0, 8) of Bott Periodicity occupies 256 R. Cl(0, 8)
may be viewed as a certain real slice of the complex Clifford algebra Cl(8). The same can be said for the 256 Rdimensional H16 (C), which we explore here.
Beyond having an appropriate capacity, one may also
require some inner substructure that will prompt the
symmetries of these algebras to break. That is, if one
opts not to rely upon arbitrarily chosen Higgs fields. It
is known that both Cl(0, 8) and e8 are closely tied to composition algebras that, in one way or another, generate
them. The same can be said for H16 (C).
In the existing literature, there is no shortage of models
built to address the three-generation problem. Notable
proposals include, but of course are not limited to [9]-[45].
We point out in particular a long-running interest in the
27-dimensional exceptional Jordan algebra, H3 (O), [9],
[14], [22], [23], [30], [33], [34], [35]. (In future work, we
will examine H3 (C ⊗ H ⊗ O), the 3×3 hermitian matrices
over C ⊗ H ⊗ O.) Also closely related are e8 models,
advocated for in [8], [29], [36], [44].
In this article, we will generate H16 (C) from the left
action of the algebra C ⊗ H ⊗ O on itself. There is a long
history of authors making use of C ⊗ H ⊗ O in various
ways, starting with Dixon. See for example, [12], [48],
[1], [20], [24], [26], [32], [37], [38], [2], [39], [40], [41], [3],
[49], [56], [44].

Of special relevance is a long-running research programme of Alvarez, Delage, Valenzuela, and Zanelli,
known as Unconventional SUSY, [46], [47]. There, a related superconnection is proposed, in the form of a Lie
superalgebra.
Also in close connection is the work of Balbino, de
Freitas, Rana, Toppan, [50], Stoilova and Van der Jeugt,
[51], Aizawa, Kuznetsova, Toppan [52], and colleagues in
the realm of Zn2 graded algebras and their applications in
physics.
This article is a distillation of a long-running research
effort. Early three-generation results appeared in [1],
[20], [24], based on the Clifford algebra Cl(6), generated
by the left action of the complex octonions on themselves.
Already in [1] a proposal was put forward to extend to
Cl(8) by including the quaternions. From here, it was
suggested to take some real slice of Cl(8), be it as the
Bott Periodic Cl(0, 8), e.g. [2], [38], [3], or as the hermitian slice H16 (C), e.g. [32], [38],[39], [40]. Numerous
seminars may be found online.
III.

CONTENT

In Section IV we describe the Standard Model’s particle content. In Section V we introduce the physically
relevant Euclidean Jordan algebras H2 (C) and H16 (C).
In Section VI we describe how H16 (C) can be generated
by C⊗H⊗O, via its left multiplication algebra, LC⊗H⊗O ,
which we define.
In Section VII we summarize early three-generation
results.
In Section VIII, we define a Peirce decomposition of
H16 (C), based on division algebraic substructure. Symmetries respecting this division algebraic substructure
are then identified. Surviving octonionic symmetries
are found to be u(3) ⊕ u(2) ⊕ 3u(1), wherein lies the
Standard Model’s internal gauge symmetries su(3)C ⊕
su(2)L ⊕ u(1)Y . Non-redundant quaternionic symmetries
are found to be so(3), and are expected to eventually give
rise to spatial symmetry, although in an unexpected way.
Anomaly cancellation will need to be addressed in future
work.
Applying these symmetries to H16 (C) leads to an interpretation of the algebra as a space of Weyl super operators. We find a (cursory) momentum-space covariant derivative of dimension 64 R, and three generations,
each of dimension 64 R. There is no overlap between
these spaces, so that one finds H16 (C) partitioned as
64 R + 3 · 64 R = 256 R. The third generation lacks only
those representations involving the top quark.
In this section, we furthermore propose an alternative
product description for the third generation, motivated
by Penrose’s early work, and a recent article on triality [44].
We identify the Weyl operators pµ σ µ as possible dark
matter candidates.
In Section IX we demonstrate how H16 (C) and our

## PDF page 3

3
Peirce decomposition induce a Z2 grading, hence forming
a superalgebra from the Standard Model’s local degrees
of freedom. We then show how the grading can in fact be
extended to Z52 . As one special case, we demonstrate how
multiplication yields the action of the covariant derivative
on fermion states. As another special case, representations corresponding to gluons and W bosons are formed
by multiplying quark representations together.
In Section X, we identify sets of Peirce idempotents
as projective measurements. Furthermore, we identify a
certain algebra of observables as the algebra of real linear
combinations of Peirce idempotents. Of special interest
for us is the fact that the Peirce idempotents defined in
this article preclude the observation of colour.
In Section XI, we point out that spatial so(3) = su(2)
symmetries arise in precisely the same way that internal symmetries do. Perhaps this could have meaningful
implications for quantum gravity, e.g. [53]. We mention that even though Lorentzian symmetry does not
arise as a unitary symmetry on the Jordan algebra, it
does appear nonetheless since the proper orthochronous
group SO+ (3,1) constitutes the non-trivial inner automorphisms of C ⊗ H. It is this group of inner automorphisms, in fact, that establishes a complementary relationship between generations (mass) and spin in the
model.
The existence of an independent so(3) symmetry for
each diagonal Peirce block leads to an interpretation that
fermions be understood as extended objects. However,
with this said, one finds no need to introduce a background spacetime for this model.
It is pointed out that the set of all fermion representations in this model may be represented by the complete
graph K5 . It is proposed that this feature, in addition to
Jordan algebraic structure, may eventually enable this
model of particle physics to be understood in terms of
quantum computing. Please see [67], [68], [69], [63], [70],
[71].
After the conclusion, we describe areas of current development. In Section XIII we describe how extending to
C⊗H⊗O’s full multiplication algebra allows this current
work to fit into a wider research program known as Bott
Periodic Particle Physics, [2], [3]. It also opens the door
to a description of gravity via generalized tetrads. In Section XIV, we propose that the Peirce decomposition seen
in Figure 5 may ultimately stem from the little-known
15 R dimensional algebra O ⊕ H ⊕ C ⊕ R, or further, the
nested Cayley-Dickson embeddings R ⊂ C ⊂ H ⊂ O ⊂ S.

It is something of an enigma that in the decades since
its inception, experiment has hardly nudged the Standard
Model from its original particle content. Notably in 2015
the Nobel prize was awarded to Super-Kamiokande and
the Sudbury Neutrino Observatory for the discovery of
neutrino oscillations. The finding fares well with the possibility of the existence of right-handed neutrinos: what
would be a first, and frankly, instinctive correction to the
theory’s particle content. The corresponding irreducible
representations for these particles are found below.
Sterile neutrinos (complex representations)

SU(3)C ×SU(2)L ×U(1)Y /Z6 , and spacetime symmetries
of SL(2,C), or more precisely, the Poincaré group with
its Lorentz subgroup extended to the double cover. Its
degrees of freedom are said to reside within the vector

At this point, we have introduced the Jordan algebra
meant to describe the Standard Model’s local degrees of
freedom. However, on its own, H16 (C) is largely featureless. In the next section, we will demonstrate how one
can generate H16 (C) in a way that imparts on it useful
substructure needed to distinguish particle irreps. Explicitly, we introduce certain normed division algebras,
and their multiplication algebras, although it should be
noted that this article can still be understood simply in
terms of hermitian matrices.
VI.

H16 (C) AS GENERATED BY THE
DIVISION ALGEBRAS
A.

Definition of A = C ⊗ H ⊗ O

All tensor products will be over R throughout this article, unless otherwise stated.
As emphasized earlier, the algebra of complex quaternions, C ⊗ H, is isomorphic to the algebra of 2×2 complex matrices, and hence is highly relevant in theoretical
physics. We will write the usual C-basis for C ⊗ H as
{ϵ0 , ϵ1 , ϵ2 , ϵ3 }. Here, ϵ0 = 1, ϵ2j = −1 for j ∈ {1, 2, 3},
and ϵ1 ϵ2 = ϵ3 , plus cyclic permutations. These last relations may be captured more succinctly as
ϵa ϵb = −δab + εabc ϵc

(18)

for a, b, c ∈ {1, 2, 3}, where εabc is the usual totally antisymmetric tensor with ε123 = 1. We will take i to denote
the complex imaginary unit as usual.
It is less well-known that the complex octonions, C⊗O,
are also relevant for theoretical physics. See for example
Günaydin and Gürsey’s early work on quarks [54]. We

will write the usual C-basis for the complex octonions,
C ⊗ O, as {e0 , e1 , . . . , e7 }. Here, e0 = 1 and
ei ej = −δij + fijk ek ,

(19)

for i, j, k ∈ {1, 2, . . . 7}. Analogously, fijk is a totally anti-symmetric tensor with fijk = 1 when ijk ∈
{124, 235, 346, 457, 561, 672, 713}. The remaining values
of fijk are determined by anti-symmetry, and vanish otherwise.
We can now join C ⊗ H and C ⊗ O together in a tensor
product over C. This gives A := (C ⊗ H) ⊗C (C ⊗ O) =
C ⊗ H ⊗ O. Multiplication of elements in A is defined
by setting (x1 ⊗C y1 )(x2 ⊗C y2 ) = x1 x2 ⊗C y1 y2 for all
x1 , x2 ∈ C ⊗ H, and y1 , y2 ∈ C ⊗ O. A C-basis for A
is given by {ϵj ⊗ ek | j ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3}, k ∈ {0, 1, . . . , 7}}.
Readers can then confirm that A is a 32 C-dimensional
non-commutative, non-associative algebra.
For
∑ convenience, we will write arbitrary elements of A
as j,k cjk ϵj ek for cjk ∈ C, now omitting the ⊗ symbol.
We see that ϵj ek = ek ϵj for all j, k. As an example
calculation, consider a, b ∈ A with a = 4ϵ1 e2 and b =
(5 + i) ϵ2 e4 . Then ab = (20 + 4i) ϵ3 e1 .
Recently it was shown how A = C ⊗ H ⊗ O can encode
a full generation of Standard Model particles, [37], [38].
B.

Definition of A’s multiplication algebras

With this said, our ultimate goal is to encode not only a
single generation, but rather, the Standard Model’s three
generations, together with its gauge and Higgs bosons.
How might one leverage C ⊗ H ⊗ O to somehow accommodate so many more degrees of freedom?
Inspired by nature’s genetic code, [3], let us consider
sequences of C ⊗ H ⊗ O elements left-multiplying themselves.
Recall that Cl(8) is the 256-dimensional unital associative algebra over C generated by γ1 , . . . , γ8 , satisfying
the relations
{γj , γk } = 2δjk

(20)

for all j, k. Given x ∈ A, we write Lx for the complexlinear map A → A given by
Lx (y) := xy

(21)

for all y ∈ A.
We define A’s multiplication algebra, LA , to be the
subalgebra of EndC (A) generated by {Lx | x ∈ A}. It is
straightforward to show that LA is isomorphic to Cl(8).
To be explicit, one may take the eight elements
{ Liej , Lϵ1 e7 , Lϵ2 e7 }

(22)

for j ∈ {1, 2, . . . , 6} as a generating set. As an example calculation in LA , consider the multiplication of
two elements A, B ∈ End(A) with A = 4Lϵ1 e2 and

## PDF page 6

6
B = (5 + i) Lϵ2 e4 . Then AB := A ◦ B = (20 + 4i) Lϵ1 e2 ◦
Lϵ2 e4 = (20 + 4i) Lϵ3 e2 ◦ Le4 ̸= (20 + 4i) Lϵ3 e1 .
For later use, we may define A’s right multiplication
algebra, RA , to be the subalgebra of EndC (A) generated
by {Rx | x ∈ A}, where Rx (y) := yx. Finally, we define
A’s full multiplication algebra, MA , to be the subalgebra
of EndC (A) generated by both {Lx | x ∈ A} and {Rx′ |
x′ ∈ A}. It can be confirmed that MA ≃ EndC (A).
Given x ∈ LA , we write x∗ for the complex conjugate of x that maps i ↦→ −i. We also define an antiautomorphism LA → LA , denoted x ↦→ x† , by i ↦→ −i,
Lϵj ↦→ −Lϵj for j ∈ {1, 2, 3}, Lek ↦→ −Lek for k ∈
{1, . . . , 7}, with (xy)† = y † x† for all x, y ∈ LA . We call
x† the hermitian conjugate of x.
In this article, we will be most interested in the hermitian subset of LA , that is, HLA := {h ∈ LA | h = h† }.
It is possible to show that HLA forms an algebra under
the Jordan product m(a, b) = ab + ba = ab + ba† for
a, b ∈ HLA , and that this algebra is isomorphic to the
16×16 hermitian matrices, H16 (C).
We will find that HLC⊗H encodes the representations
of spacetime symmetries, while HLC⊗O encodes the representations of internal symmetries. This identification
allows for easy compliance with the Coleman-Mandula
theorem.

distinct ways to generate LO ≃ M8 (R). They correspond to Clifford algebras Cl(0, 6), Cl(3, 3), and
Cl(4, 2). Not only can Cl(0, 6) lead to Clifford volume
elements Le7 and Re7 , as described above, but so can
Cl(4, 2). Consider, for example Cl(4, 2) as generated by
{ Le2 , Le6 , Le1 Le3 Le4 , Le1 Le3 Le5 , Le1 Le4 Le5 , Le3 Le4 Le5 }.
Of course, this signature is of special importance due to
its connection to conformal symmetry in 3+1 D.
These Clifford volume elements allow us to define a set
of idempotents,

s∗ := 12 (1 − iLe7 )

Peirce decomposition, which we will define more carefully
in the context of HLA .

EARLY THREE GENERATION RESULTS
A.

Three generation su(3)C structure

Finding mathematical objects with natural threegeneration structure has, over the years, persistently
proven a challenge. With this said, some preliminary
QCD results were found in [1], [20], [24].
The algebra in which this su(3)C structure was identified is the left multiplication algebra of the complex
octonions, LC⊗O . Readers can confirm that LC⊗O is isomorphic to the complex Clifford algebra Cl(6), in addition to being isomorphic to EndC (C ⊗ O). It may be
generated by the set {Lej } for j ∈ {1 . . . 6}. In this case,
it can be shown that the linear map Le7 coincides with
Le1 Le2 Le3 Le4 Le5 Le6 , known as the volume element of
the Clifford algebra.
Now incidentally, this same octonionic imaginary unit,
e7 , provides us with another Clifford volume element,
Re7 , when Cl(6) is generated by the set {Rej } for j ∈
{1 . . . 6}. That is, Re1 Re2 Re3 Re4 Re5 Re6 = −Re7 . Note
that Re7 ̸= Le7 . The elements Lie7 and Rie7 may be
thought of as generalizations of the chirality operator
γ 5 := iγ 0 γ 1 γ 2 γ 3 in the Dirac algebra. As we will see,
they supply the division algebraic substructure that defines the su(3)C particle representations.
It should be noted that {Lej } and {Rej } for
j ∈ {1 . . . 6} are not the only generators capable
of producing Le7 and Re7 as volume elements. In
keeping with the well-known table of Clifford matrix
algebras, e.g. [55] or [20] p. 20, there are three

VIII. STANDARD MODEL
REPRESENTATIONS INSIDE H16 (C)

Finally we are ready to upgrade LC⊗O ≃ Cl(6) to the
full LC⊗H⊗O ≃ Cl(8) algebra, and to take its hermitian

## PDF page 8

8
part, HLA ≃ H16 (C). We will see that the Clifford volume elements {Le7 , Re7 , Le2 Le6 Le7 } induce a splitting
of HLA into a set of vector spaces that mirror, to a large
extent, familiar Standard Model irreps. More formally,
this splitting is known as a Peirce decomposition. See
Figure (5) for a preview.
A.

A natural question to ask at this point is: What motivates these particular S4 and S5 Peirce sets? At the
moment we will mention that S4 derives from identifying
certain complex structures within the octonionic sector,
while Sa and Sā derive from identifying a certain quaternionic structure. Please see Sections VII C and XIV for
relevant proposals.

We label these idempotents as s1 := sS, s2 := sS ,

can be confirmed that this is a set of mutually orthogonal idempotents that sum to 1.
Readers will find later in this article that the change
from S4 to S5 indeed induces the electroweak decomposition of expression (32). This change in Peirce sets
induces a further partitioning of the Peirce blocks in Figure (1). We will make those new partition lines known
by depicting them in blue. Relevant figures where these
blue partition lines may be seen include Figures (2), (3),
(4), (5).
Beyond isolating quaternionic subspaces within the octonions, we point out that Sa and Sā idempotents help
encode a certain i su(5) algebra within the H22 , H23 , H33
blocks of Figure 5. See [57] and [58]. (It should be
noted that Kaushal Kumar participated in a calculation
of these idempotents in the context of a related noncommutative geometry project.)

set S2 :=pµ{s, s. ∗ }, then we would interpret these
representations as bosonic. In this case, our model would
indeed have a 1:1 boson-to-fermion ratio.
However, from the S5 perspective, one finds a set of
representations, which, in every case, aligns with a third
generation fermion. In fact, one finds particle irreps for
each of the lightest third generation states. This sector is seen to be missing two known Standard Model
irreps, namely, (tL , bL ) and tR . These coincide exactly
with those irreps needed to describe the top quark.

At this point, we have identified all third generation
representations, with exception to those irreps involving
the top quark, and also a possible third generation sterile
neutrino. We are furthermore missing the Higgs. On
the other hand, the final Peirce block we have yet to
describe is H34 . This H34 is unique within the Peirce
decomposition because it is the only block that is bosonic
with respect to S4 , and fermionic with respect to S5 .

We observe that this final Peirce block transforms as
a product of the Higgs and sterile anti-neutrino (or neu-

## PDF page 13

13
trino), and so we will take the liberty to describe it here
in that way. It should be noted that H34 and H35 correspond to essentially the same representation, and so
there may be some freedom of interpretation for these
two Peirce blocks.
This now completes the particle identification of our
Peirce blocks. One finds, curiously, that the light third
generation fermions are described in duplicate. It is not
clear whether or not this trait need be detectable.
So, how might one account for the existence of these
heaviest top and bottom quarks? On one hand, it is
plausible that the top quark could be composite, while
the bottom quark could be partially composite. Indeed,
there is precedence for such proposals, [5], [6], [7].
As an alternative perspective, one might postulate that
the third generation states could ultimately enter as a
product, reminiscent of division algebraic triality, proposed in [44]. In [44], the Standard Model’s three generations were described as a triality triple, with the vector
representation entering in as products of third generation

This provides one way for the full set of third generation
states, including top and bottom quarks, to embed into
the algebra, albeit as a product. To a certain extent, it
mirrors structure native to the spinor-helicity formalism.
These particular combinations of products were chosen
specifically because they align with the way that the first
and second generation fermions mix in under Jordan algebraic multiplication. Worth noting here is the fact that
it is possible in this model to multiply two fermions in
order to obtain another fermion. This is a direct consequence of our definition of fermions as off-diagonal elements within the Peirce decomposition. For more on this
topic, please see Section IX.

A Jordan algebraic mosaic

In summary, within H16 (C) we find irreducible representations corresponding to a (cursory) momentum-space
covariant derivative, including gluons and electroweak
bosons on the diagonal. We find two full generations
of fermions within the outer off-diagonals blocks. Finally, within the inner-off-diagonal blocks, we find the
fermion representations for a third generation, with only
the heaviest particles evading a simple description. We
identify a possible inclusion of a third sterile neutrino
and the Higgs. Please see Figure 5.
It is worth noting a couple of interesting ratios that
arise in counting the Standard Model’s states. In this
model, we have found a 3:1 ratio of the fermioinic sector
relative to the boson sector. This counting was made possible in part by the inclusion of separate Weyl operators
pµ σ µ for the various particle representations. Recently,
it was pointed out by Jorge Zanelli that when the Higgs
and Weyl operators are excluded, the fermionic ratio becomes 4:1.
In contrast to the Standard Model’s usual counting,
we find that the Weyl operators pµ σ µ appear as distinct
degrees of freedom. That is before any constraints are
applied to the H16 (C) algebra. It is worth investigating
whether these additional Weyl operators may in fact be
dark matter candidates.

A Z52 -GRADED ALGEBRA

At this point, we see how H16 (C) provides a representation space for su(3)C ⊕ su(2)L ⊕ u(1)Y and so(3)
symmetries. But in what sense is this a superalgebra?
Thanks to the Peirce decomposition earlier described,
the algebra HLA , together with S5 , facilitate not only a
Z2 -graded algebra, but furthermore, a Z52 -graded algebra.
We will define a Zn2 -graded algebra as an algebra A
with vector space decomposition

In particular, a (Z2 -graded) superalgebra can be
achieved, as originally claimed, when we coarse grain S5
to a simpler set defined by S2 := { s, s∗ }, for example.
According to this grading, one may identify A0 as bosonic

In contrast to S2 , the S5 case finally enables a 3:1
fermion to boson ratio. With this fine-grained Peirce
decomposition, the diagonal blocks constitute
A(0,0,0,0,0) = H11 ⊕ H22 ⊕ H33 ⊕ H44 ⊕ H55 ,

Readers may appreciate an interesting feature of this
model. Namely, a particle’s identification as fermionic

or bosonic depends on which Peirce decomposition is enabled. One might even postulate that in the early universe, the Peirce decomposition was more coarse grained,
as in S2 , and bosonic degrees of freedom had a larger
presence.

X.

PEIRCE PROJECTIVE MEASUREMENTS
AND AN ALGEBRA OF OBSERVABLES

Physicists may recognize immediately the similarity
between a set of Peirce idempotents, Sn , and the familiar
projective measurement of quantum theory (a projectionvalued measure). A projective measurement is also described by a set of orthogonal projection operators that
sum to one.
It is straightforward to see that the set of idempotents
S5 may be fine grained further by specifying one quaternionic imaginary unit (Clifford volume element) for each
block. That is, S5 = {sS, sS ∗ , . . . } may be fine-grained
to become S10 := {sS 12 (1 + iϵj ), sS 21 (1 − iϵj )}, sS ∗ 12 (1 +
iϵj ′ ), sS ∗ 12 (1 − iϵj ′ ), . . . }. Such a set would allow for a
measurement of helicity (spin). As an aside, we mention
that a set of such idempotents generates a poset under
addition so that coarse-grained projection operators may
be constructed from fine-grained ones.
Beyond projective measurements, one finds that a certain algebra of observables, A, follows naturally from Sn .
That is, consider the vector space A given by all real
linear combinations of the idempotents sk in Sn .
a = rk sk ,

for a ∈ A and rk ∈ R.

(57)

Elements in this vector space close under Jordan multiplication. Hence, we identify A as a certain local algebra
of observables, [40], identifiable as the superselection or
classical observables of [64].
Now, readers may appreciate that the choice of imaginary units used in S5 and S10 were not unique. Although
we have arbitrarily chosen e7 to define s and S, octonionic imaginary units reside on the six-sphere, S 6 , and
so Sn may be expected to vary along different points in
an emergent spacetime. Similarly, quaternionic imaginary units reside on the two-sphere, S 2 . See for example,
[39], [40], and recent work by Hun Jang, [65].
This algebra of observables, A, is of particular importance in this article. That is, readers should notice that
the Peirce decompositions S5 and S10 , derived from division algebraic volume elements, preclude colour as an
observable quantity.
We propose investigating the idea that A(x), supported by a suitably-defined connection, could comprise
a full algebra of observables for the system.

XI.

ON SPACETIME SYMMETRIES

It may be clear to readers that quite a different physical picture emerges from this model, as compared to the
standard QFT. This is largely due to the multiple independent so(3) symmetries of equation (45) found in the
quaternionic sector of the algebra. We note the following:
1. At first glance, we find so(3), not so(3, 1).
It is perhaps a feature of this model that spatial symmetries enter the picture via equation (45) in exact analogy to internal symmetries.
With this said, this does not mean that so(3, 1) is
excluded from the model. Lorentzian symmetry appears naturally, given that the proper orthochronous
group, SO+ (3,1), comprises the non-trivial inner automorphisms of C ⊗ H. (Furthermore, it should be pointed
out that so(3, 1) provides the reduced structure algebra
for H2 (C).) Hence, in agreement with [63], [20], [3], [66]
we propose that Lorentzian structure need not originate
from a spacetime manifold. Avoiding dependence on a
spacetime manifold might prove fruitful in the context
of quantum gravity [40], and could evade the inevitability of a block universe, which does not align with our
experience of time.
2. The model exhibits a non-trivial relationship between generations (mass) and spin.
As can be confirmed in Section VIII, this model represents an excitation from one generation to another analogously to how it represents an excitation from one spin
state to another. With this said, these two properties
are related to one another non-trivially. That is, as mentioned above, C ⊗ H is subject to inner automorphisms
of the form LG RG−1 for G ∈ SL(2,C). In practice, this
means that invertible operators in the generation action
can effectively be transported into the spin action on the
other side, and vice versa, via a judicious choice of G. It
would be of interest to investigate whether such a property could have implications for neutrino oscillations.
3. There is an independent so(3) symmetry for each
sj ∈ Sn .
In this model, we propose characterizing fermions as
having two distinct endpoints ({si , sj } with i ̸= j). In
this sense, we view a fermion as an extended object (representable as an edge) within the Jordan algebra. It need
not be embedded in any background spacetime.
As mentioned earlier, we may think of ∑
spinors in this
model as the “square root” of pµ σ µ =
ΨΨ† operators. Of particular interest will be the usual symmetric
cone associated with this Euclidean Jordan algebra, as it
generalizes the forward light cone in 3+1 dimensions.
This characterization of fermions, via asymmetric
idempotents, allows for a possibility not available in most
spinorial theories. That is, the product of two distinct
fermions may result in a third fermion. Such a property
could be crucial in the description of three generations
as a triality triple. Unlike in [44], all three generations
could then admit a fermionic characterization.
On the other hand, gauge bosons have common end-

## PDF page 17

17
Cl(2) can be viewed as being the most common. That is,
Bott Periodicity tells us that the vast majority of nondegenerate real Clifford algebras Cl(p, q) can be decomposed as matrix algebras in the form:
Cl(p, q) = Cl(p0 , q0 ) ⊗ Cl(0, 8) ⊗ Cl(0, 8) ⊗ Cl(0, 8) . . .
= Cl(p0 , q0 ) ⊗ Cl(0, 8)⊗m

(58)
for some large m. Here, p0 = p mod 8 and q0 = q mod 8.
That is, internally, these Clifford algebras are overwhelmingly dominated by factors of Cl(0, 8).
Similarly, the vast majority of non-degenerate complex
Clifford algebras Cl(n) can be decomposed as matrix algebras in the form:
Cl(n) = Cl(n0 ) ⊗ Cl(2) ⊗ Cl(2) ⊗ Cl(2) . . .
= Cl(n0 ) ⊗ Cl(2)⊗m

(59)

for some large m. Here, n0 = n mod 2. Internally, these
Clifford algebras are overwhelmingly dominated by factors of Cl(2). Readers may appreciate that these Cl(2)
factors may be interpreted as qubit algebras.
The Tenfold Way, [72], [73], is tied to these repeating structures, and, we speculate, may very well underlie
elementary particle physics, [3].
Now, one motivation for studying the algebra R ⊗ C ⊗
H ⊗ O came from the finding that C ⊗ H ≃ R ⊗ C ⊗ H ≃
Cl(2) concisely describes each of the known Lorentz representations of the Standard Model, [20], [3]. Inclusion
of the final normed division algebra, O, provides a viable
medium with which to generate internal symmetries.
However, A = R ⊗ C ⊗ H ⊗ O might also be useful for
other purposes. Namely, it generates Cl(0, 8) ⊗ Cl(2).
Consider the full multiplication algebra of A, denoted
MA , earlier defined in Section VI B. As matrix algebras,
MA , begets a factorized form,
MA ≃ Cl(10)

≃ Cl(0, 8) ⊗ Cl(2).

(60)

It is anticipated that this will be a recurring structure
within the construction of a Bott Periodic Fock space, as
described in [3].

In this article, we have described something close to
the Standard Model’s particle spectrum within the first

with H16 (C) given by the hermitian part of LA ⊂ MA .
H8 (C) is octonionic, and describes internal degrees of
freedom, while H2 (C) is quaternionic, and describes
spin/helicity. These H16 (C) ≃ HLA (C) degrees of freedom are often associated with the fibre in the Standard
Model, when viewed as a QFT.
One may interpret H2 (C) as a set of 4 R dimensional
objects of the form ∼ pµ σ µ , that is, with coefficients of
a single spacetime index. Extending this, one may iden˜ 2 (C) as describing an object with two

From these beginnings, we aim to produce an emergent
theory of gravity, free from an a priori notion of spacetime, [20]. The full Jordan algebra HMA (C) ≃ H32 (C)
may be understood as an algebra of extended vierbeins
for the Standard Model.
XIV.

OUTLOOK: O ⊕ H ⊕ C ⊕ R

One long-standing challenge in this construction has
been to find the origin of the quaternionic idempotents
of our Peirce decomposition. See Section VII C.
Here we propose a solution by reframing this model in
terms of the 15 R dimensional algebra
O ⊕ H ⊕ C ⊕ R.

Specifically, one may consider the subalgebras of those
multiplication algebras (endomorphisms) of R, C, H, O
commuting with a complex structure specified by the
volume element of their corresponding Clifford algebras.
Specifically,

where we have taken care to account for both left- and

It is important to note that the quaternionic case
above differs significantly from the others, since it comprises the unique division algebra whose left- and rightmultiplication algebras lead to distinct sets of endomorphisms. As such, one may view its endomorphisms as

## PDF page 18

18
EndR (H) ≃ Cl(0, 2) ⊗ Cl(0, 2). Reducing each factor to
its even subalgebra gives
EndR (H) ≃ Cl(0, 2) ⊗ Cl(0, 2) ↦→ C ⊗ C ≃ C ⊕ C.
(66)
One may anticipate this fact to be the source of electroweak symmetry breaking.
We propose to study these structures in the context of
Connes’ non-commutative geometry, [74], which is canonically based on the algebra M3 (C) ⊕ H ⊕ C.

common with that of Figure (5). Notably, one finds this
time a counting suggestive of on-shell states. (Transverse polarizations of gauge bosons may be enabled by
the auxiliary imaginary units used in the Cayley-Dickson
process.)
The details in these final sections are to be carefully
verified, and a complete bibliography of earlier related
work is to be compiled.

OUTLOOK: R ⊂ C ⊂ H ⊂ O ⊂ S

The research directions of Bott Periodic Particle
Physics and of O ⊕ H ⊕ C ⊕ R modules may in fact merge
when this model is reframed in terms of the 16 R dimensional sedenion algebra, S. It is known that EndR (S) ≃
Cl(0, 8). Furthermore, S admits a vector space decomposition as
S ↦→ e8 O ⊕ e5 H ⊕ e6 C ⊕ e7 R ⊕ R,

(67)

for e8 ∈ S.
This may be seen to encode the nested inclusions
R⊂C⊂H⊂O⊂S
according to the Cayley-Dickson process.
Repeating the procedure from the previous section
leads to a set of diagonal blocks C ⊕ M3 (C) ⊕ M2 (C) ⊕
C ⊕ R ⊕ R. The particle content in this case has much in
